# 02 — Static Audit

> Diverse-lens audit promoted through an adversarial falsifier to a fixpoint (3 round(s)). Coverage: 609/609 required files visited. Severity mix — critical: 1 · high: 11 · medium: 69 · low: 130 · info: 37.

| cosine-validator-wrong-file | bug | curricula/cs336_a1/modules/cosine_schedule/validator.sh:18 | Line 18: `cp cs336_basics/optimizer.py "$SHADOW_WORKTREE/cs336_basics/optimizer.py"`. The test suite imports `get_lr_cosine_schedule` from `cs336_basics.utils` (confirmed: `tests/adapters.py:11-15` does `from cs336_basics.utils import ... get_lr_cosine_schedule as _get_lr_cosine_schedule_impl`). The developer reference implementation lives in `modes/developer/cs336_basics/utils.py:75`. The validator copies only `optimizer.py` — it never propagates the student's `utils.py` to the shadow worktree, so `test_optimizer.py::test_lr_cosine_schedule` always runs against the stale/original `utils.py`. Cosine-schedule validation is permanently broken regardless of correct student implementation. | Doubly confirmed. (1) get_lr_cosine_schedule is defined in cs336_basics/utils.py (dev:75, student:61) and imported from cs336_basics.utils at tests/adapters.py:11-15, but validator.sh:18 copies only optimizer.py, so student utils.py never propagates. (2) validator.sh:33/37/40 invoke ::test_lr_cosine_schedule, but tests/test_optimizer.py:52 defines test_get_lr_cosine_schedule, so pytest collects nothing. Critical. |

| text-generation-nucleus-sampling-mask-inverted | doc_code_drift | curricula/cs336_a1/modules/text_generation/build_prompt.txt:310-323 | The nucleus sampling algorithm description at lines 138-144 correctly states: 'Keep until cumsum >= p: [A, B, C] (cumsum = 0.95)', meaning we retain the lowest-index tokens that together accumulate probability mass p. However, the implementation code at line 311 sets 'mask = cumsum_probs >= p', which marks positions where the cumulative sum has already exceeded p (i.e., the later, lower-probability tokens). Then 'filtered_probs[~mask] = 0.0' zeroes out the positions NOT in this mask, discarding the early high-probability tokens and keeping the tail instead. With sorted_probs=[0.6, 0.25, 0.10, 0.03, 0.02] and p=0.9, the correct result keeps indices 0,1,2; the provided code (after setting mask[0]=True) keeps indices 0,2,3,4 — the opposite of what nucleus sampling requires. A student following this code example will implement a functionally incorrect sampler. | build_prompt.txt:311 'mask = cumsum_probs >= p', :313 mask[0]=True, :316 filtered_probs[~mask]=0. For sorted [0.6,0.25,0.10,0.03,0.02], p=0.9, cumsum=[.6,.85,.95,.98,1.0] -> mask=[F,F,T,T,T]->[T,F,T,T,T]; zeroes only B, keeps A,C,D,E — opposite of documented 'Keep [A,B,C]' (:142). Functionally wrong reference sampler; high. |

| unsandboxed-student-code-execution | design_defect | engine/validator.py:108 | Lines 108-116: subprocess.run([str(validator_path.resolve())], cwd=..., env=env, timeout=300, check=False). The validator shell scripts import and run student Python code via '$MASTERY_PYTHON -m pytest'. There is no container, namespace, seccomp filter, or rlimit applied before or during execution. Student code runs with the full privileges of the invoking user process. A malicious student implementation can read arbitrary files, write outside the workspace, make network calls, install packages, or fork-bomb (partial mitigation: 5-minute wall-clock timeout exists but does not limit CPU/memory/fd/child-process counts). Provisional intent states this is local-only, but no security boundary separates student code from the user's home directory and credentials. | Confirmed validator.py:108-116 subprocess.run with only timeout, env, cwd — no namespace/seccomp/rlimit. Curriculum shell+python runs with full user privileges. Medium fits a local tool executing third-party curriculum content; cannot refute. |

| memory-limit-decorator-broken-for-generators | bug | tests/test_tokenizer.py:450 | Lines 449-455: `_encode_iterable` contains `yield from`, making it a generator function. The `memory_limit` decorator's wrapper calls `result = f(*args, **kwargs)` which, for a generator function, immediately returns a generator object without executing any code — then `finally` resets RLIMIT_AS before any iteration begins. As a result, `test_encode_iterable_memory_usage` (line 416-428) iterates the generator with no memory limit in effect. The 1 MB memory constraint the test claims to enforce is never actually applied to the encode_iterable call path. The test can pass for a memory-inefficient implementation. | Confirmed _encode_iterable (test_tokenizer.py:449-455) is a generator (`yield from`) decorated @memory_limit; wrapper (21-32) does `result=f(...)` returning the generator unexecuted, then resets RLIMIT_AS in finally before the test iterates it at 427. The 1MB cap never applies to encode_iterable, so the test passes for memory-inefficient impls. Real logic bug, medium. |
| bpe-merge-correctness-assertion-commented-out | intent_mismatch | tests/test_train_bpe.py:54 | Line 54: `# assert merges == reference_merges  # Too strict - commented out`. The original test intent was to verify exact BPE merge order against a reference. The comment at lines 51-53 states 'Our implementation uses (count, earliest_index, lexicographic) tie-breaking / Reference uses different tie-breaking, causing divergence at index 64 / Both are correct - just different valid orderings'. The claim 'both are correct' is asserted in prose but not formally verified. The replacement assertions (lines 57-58: `assert len(merges) >= 243` and `assert len(merges) <= 245`) only check count, not merge identity or ordering. A buggy implementation that produces the right number of wrong merges will pass this test. | Confirmed test_train_bpe.py:54 `# assert merges == reference_merges  # Too strict - commented out`; replacements at 57-58 only bound count. No assertion on merge identity/order remains, so a wrong-but-right-count implementation passes. Medium intent_mismatch. |

The `memory_limit` decorator (lines 19-35) calls `result = f(*args, **kwargs)` which, for a generator function, returns a generator object immediately without running any body. The `finally` block at line 33 then resets the memory limit back to its prior value before any token is produced. When `test_encode_iterable_memory_usage` (lines 416-429) subsequently iterates `for _id in _encode_iterable(tokenizer, f):`, the 1 MB RLIMIT_AS constraint has already been lifted. The test therefore provides no memory-usage guarantee — `encode_iterable` could buffer the entire corpus and still pass. The test makes no explicit assertion either (no `assert` in lines 416-429), so it passes vacuously on Linux, giving false confidence in memory-efficient streaming tokenization. | Confirmed: memory_limit (test_tokenizer.py:19-36) does result=f(...) then resets RLIMIT_AS in finally; _encode_iterable (449-455) is a generator (yield from) so f() returns immediately without running the body and the limit lifts before iteration; test_encode_iterable_memory_usage (420-428) iterates afterward with no assert. The 1MB cap is never enforced; test passes vacuously. Medium. |
| order-sensitive-comparison-set-outputs | intent_mismatch | curricula/cp_accelerator/patterns/backtracking/problems/lc_78/validator.sh:38 | lc_78/validator.sh line 38: `if result == expected:` uses strict equality. The LeetCode 78 problem statement explicitly allows the output in any order. A correct implementation using a different traversal (e.g., iterative, bit-manipulation) that produces subsets in a different order would be marked FAIL despite being correct. Same issue in lc_90/validator.sh:38 (Subsets II), lc_46/validator.sh:38 (Permutations), lc_47/validator.sh:38 (Permutations II). Contrast with lc_1/validator.sh:52-53 which correctly sorts both sides before comparing indices. The provisional intent requires validators that correctly assess learner solutions — order-sensitive equality violates that for set-valued problems. | Confirmed: lc_78/validator.sh:38 'if result == expected:' strict equality for a problem whose output order is unconstrained; contrast lc_1/validator.sh:49-52 which sorts before comparing. A correct alternate-order traversal is false-negatived. Real intent mismatch for set-valued problems; medium. |

| generate-module-unescaped-bash-in-fstring | bug | scripts/generate_module.py:380 | Line 380 inside an f-string: `${MASTERY_PYTHON:-python3} << 'EOF'`. At runtime, evaluating this f-string raises `NameError: name 'MASTERY_PYTHON' is not defined`. The correct escape is `${{MASTERY_PYTHON:-python3}}` (double-braces produce literal `{` and `}` in f-string output). Every call to `create_validator_template()` crashes before writing any validator.sh. | Confirmed: create_validator_template (generate_module.py:364) returns an f-string; line 380 has single-brace ${MASTERY_PYTHON:-python3}, a replacement field evaluating undefined name MASTERY_PYTHON (format spec '-python3'), while line 370's ${{BASH_SOURCE[0]}} is correctly escaped. Every call raises NameError before writing validator.sh. Medium. |

| gradient-clipping-symptom-incorrect-l2-norm-math | doc_code_drift | curricula/cs336_a1/modules/gradient_clipping/bugs/per_parameter_clipping_symptom.txt:7-19 | Lines 7-8 set 'param1.grad = torch.ones(10) * 8.0  # norm = 8.0' and 'param2.grad = torch.ones(10) * 2.0  # norm = 2.0'. The actual L2 norm of torch.ones(10)*8.0 is 8*sqrt(10) ≈ 25.3, not 8.0. Line 16 then says 'Global norm = sqrt(64 + 4) ≈ 8.25', using 64 and 4 as if they were the squared norms of the individual gradient tensors; they are not (the squared L2 norms are 640 and 40, giving global norm ≈ 26.1, not 8.25). Consequently, the derived clip_coef=0.606 and final values 4.85/1.21 are all wrong. The example is internally self-consistent only if the tensors had size (1,) not (10,). As written, the comment and all downstream arithmetic are incorrect for the given code. | per_parameter_clipping_symptom.txt:7-8 labels torch.ones(10)*8.0 as 'norm = 8.0' (true L2 = 8*sqrt(10)≈25.3) and :16 'Global norm = sqrt(64+4)≈8.25' treats element values as squared norms (true ≈26.1); derived clip_coef 0.606 and outputs 4.85/1.21 all wrong for size-(10) tensors. On-topic numeric error in a gradient-norm lesson; medium. |
| swiglu-key-insight-silu-applied-to-wrong-branch | doc_code_drift | curricula/cs336_a1/modules/swiglu/build_prompt.txt:76-79 | The authoritative formula at line 56 is 'SwiGLU(x) = W₂ · (SiLU(W₁ · x) ⊙ W₃ · x)', where SiLU is applied to W₁·x (the value branch) and W₃·x is the raw gate multiplier. However, the 'Key Insight' bullet points at lines 76-79 say: 'If (W₃ · x)ᵢ is large positive → SiLU((W₃ · x)ᵢ) ≈ (W₃ · x)ᵢ → gate is open', attributing the SiLU activation to W₃·x. This is incorrect: W₃·x is never passed through SiLU in SwiGLU; it is multiplied directly with SiLU(W₁·x). The conceptual explanation confuses which branch undergoes the sigmoid-weighted activation. | swiglu/build_prompt.txt:56 formula SwiGLU=W₂·(SiLU(W₁·x) ⊙ W₃·x) applies SiLU to W₁·x, yet Key Insight :76-79 writes 'SiLU((W₃·x)ᵢ)', applying SiLU to the gate branch. | lc303-complexity-docstring-mismatch | intent_mismatch | curricula/cp_accelerator/patterns/prefix_sum/problems/lc_303/solution.py:4 | The docstring at line 4 claims 'Build: O(n), Query: O(1), Space: O(n)' but the implementation `sumRange(nums, left, right)` at line 7 rebuilds the entire prefix array on every call: `prefix = [0] * (len(nums) + 1)` / `for i in range(len(nums)): prefix[i+1] = prefix[i] + nums[i]` (lines 25-27). Each call invokes `build_unique_binary_sequences(64, L)` (line 48), which consumes the global PyTorch random state via `torch.randint`. By the time the second call occurs, the random state has been advanced by the entire first training loop (256 outer epochs × multiple batch iterations of `torch.randperm` and training ops). Consequently the two tasks are trained on entirely different sets of binary sequences, making the comparison of task difficulty non-controlled. The `torch.manual_seed(SEED)` on line 12 runs only at import time and does not re-seed before each task. If the intent is to compare inversion vs. reversal difficulty on identical input distributions, the seeding must be reset before each `make_dataset` call. | Confirmed: one_d_probes.py:12 seeds RNG only at import; make_dataset (line 48 build_unique_binary_sequences -> torch.randint) is called per task and the first task's training loop advances global RNG before the second call, so inversion vs reversal draw different X sets with no re-seed. Uncontrolled comparison. Low. |

| lc303-stateless-defeats-prefix-sum-design | intent_mismatch | curricula/cp_accelerator/patterns/prefix_sum/problems/lc_303/solution.py:7 | lc_303/solution.py line 7: `def sumRange(nums, left, right)` is a stateless function that rebuilds the full prefix array (`prefix = [0]*(len(nums)+1)` at line 25) on every single call, giving O(n) per query. LeetCode 303 (Range Sum Query — Immutable) exists specifically to teach the pattern: O(n) pre-processing once in `__init__`, O(1) answer per `sumRange` call. The correct design is a class with a stored prefix array. The validator (lc_303/validator.sh:35) calls `solve(nums=..., left=..., right=...)` on every test case, passing all three arguments each time, which structurally enforces the stateless-function design and makes it architecturally impossible for the learner to implement the intended class-based solution through the given harness. | llm-fragile-mro-check | bug | engine/services/llm_service.py:229 | Line 229: `if response_format and hasattr(response_format, '__mro__'):`. This attempts to distinguish a Pydantic model class from a plain dict/string format specifier, but `__mro__` (Method Resolution Order) exists on every Python class, not just Pydantic models. Any class passed as `response_format` — including Python built-ins, dataclasses, or custom classes — will satisfy `hasattr(cls, '__mro__')` and be routed to the `beta.chat.completions.parse()` path. The check is effectively `isinstance(response_format, type)`, which conflates 'is a class' with 'is a Pydantic model class'. A non-Pydantic class passed here will cause the `.parse()` call to fail with an opaque OpenAI API error rather than a clear type error. | Confirmed: llm_service.py:229 'hasattr(response_format, "__mro__")' is effectively isinstance(x,type) since every class has __mro__, routing any class to beta.chat.completions.parse() (line 231). Fragile; misfire requires an unusual non-Pydantic class argument. Low. |
| state-no-file-locking | design_defect | engine/state.py:1 | The `StateManager` uses atomic write-then-rename (temp file pattern for crash safety) but there is no advisory lock (e.g., `fcntl.flock`, `filelock`) around the read→modify→write cycle. The state file lives at `Path.home() / '.mastery_progress.json'` (a single file shared across all sessions), making this a real race condition in any multi-terminal workflow. | Confirmed: engine/state.py:32 single shared STATE_FILE; save() (79-87) is atomic temp-write+rename but the load->modify->save cycle has no fcntl/flock, allowing lost-update under concurrent processes. | adamw-bias-correction-comment-wrong-direction | doc_code_drift | curricula/cs336_a1/modules/adamw/bugs/missing_bias_correction.patch:12-13 | Embedded comment: `BUG: Missing bias correction! Without this, early updates are biased toward zero (10x too small at step 1), severely slowing initial training.` Numerical verification contradicts this claim. At step 1 with beta1=0.9, beta2=0.999, lr=0.001, unit gradient: WITH correction: update = lr * sign(g) = 0.001. WITHOUT correction (buggy): step_size=lr, denom=sqrt(v_1)=sqrt(0.001)*\|g\|=0.0316*\|g\|, update = lr * m_1 / (0.0316*\|g\|) = 0.001 * 0.1 / 0.0316 = 0.003162. The buggy update is 3.16x LARGER, not 10x smaller. The missing bias_correction1 makes step_size 10x smaller, but the missing 1/sqrt(bias_correction2) makes the denominator 31.6x smaller (amplifying the update), giving a net factor of 10/31.6 = 3.16x increase. The comment 'severely slowing' is the opposite of the actual effect (severely accelerating, incorrectly), giving students wrong intuition about why bias correction exists. | Re-derived from missing_bias_correction.patch:11-19. Step 1 (beta1=.9, beta2=.999, unit grad): corrected update = lr (step_size=lr/0.1, denom=sqrt(0.001)/sqrt(0.001)=1); buggy update = lr*0.1/(sqrt(0.001)+eps) ≈ 3.16*lr — i.e. ~3.16x LARGER (accelerating/destabilizing), the OPPOSITE of the comment's '10x too small at step 1, severely slowing initial training'. Comment is numerically wrong-direction. Comment-only teaching text with zero functional impact; severity already correctly at low. Survives at low. |

## INFO (37)
| ID | Class | Location | Evidence | Adversarial note |

| validator-lc1-unquoted-heredoc | security | curricula/cp_accelerator/patterns/hash_table/problems/lc_1/validator.sh:18 | Line 18 uses `${MASTERY_PYTHON:-python3} << EOF` (unquoted heredoc delimiter) whereas every other validator in the repo uses `<< 'EOF'` (single-quoted). An unquoted heredoc causes bash to expand `$var`, backtick, and `$(...)` tokens inside the Python code block before Python ever sees it. No currently-embedded Python expression is directly exploitable, but f-strings containing `$`-prefixed variable names (e.g., injected from a future edit or from environment variables embedded in test data rendered into the heredoc) would be silently expanded by bash, corrupting or injecting code. The inconsistency with all 50+ sibling validators is itself a maintenance hazard. Compare line 18 here with, e.g., `curricula/cp_accelerator/patterns/backtracking/problems/lc_78/validator.sh:17` which correctly quotes the delimiter. | Confirmed lc_1/validator.sh:18 `${MASTERY_PYTHON:-python3} << EOF` is unquoted; sibling lc_78/validator.sh:17 uses `<< 'EOF'`. Re-read body lines 19-77: no `$`, backtick, or `$(` shell-active tokens, so no live expansion — a real consistency/maintenance nit correctly floored at info. |
| cs336-validators-python-c-shell-injection | security | curricula/cs336_a1/modules/adamw/validator.sh:47 | Line 47 in adamw/validator.sh (identical pattern at line 47 in attention/validator.sh and bpe_tokenizer/validator.sh): `duration=$(python3 -c "print($end_time - $start_time)")`. The shell variables `$end_time` and `$start_time` are captured from `python3 -c 'import time; print(time.time())'` and are then interpolated without quoting into a double-quoted Python `-c` argument. In normal operation these are pure floats. However, if the Python invocation emits any extra text (warnings, PYTHONPATH messages, custom sitecustomize output), the extra content would be concatenated into a Python expression and executed. The pattern is a textbook shell-to-language injection antipattern. A safe alternative is: `duration=$(python3 -c "import time; print(${end_time} - ${start_time})")` with explicit arithmetic, or better, compute the duration entirely inside Python. | Could not refute: the unquoted float interpolation `print($end_time - $start_time)` is a genuine shell-to-language antipattern; $() captures stdout only and the operands are time.time() floats so impact is negligible — info is the correct floor. |

| ast-replacement-code-injection | security | engine/ast_harden/pattern_matcher.py:369 | Lines 369 and 422: in _create_replacement(), when replacement_def['type'] is 'replace_value_with' or 'replace_with' and source_path does not start with 'node.', the code calls 'ast.parse(source_path, mode="eval")' where source_path is the raw string from the bug definition JSON field 'replacement.source'. The resulting AST node is grafted into the student's source file, which is then written to disk and executed by pytest. A curriculum author who controls bug JSON files can inject arbitrary Python expressions into the student's code, which will execute during harden validation. Note: ast.parse() itself does not execute code, but the resulting file is run by pytest immediately after writing. Curriculum authors are trusted, but this is an unguarded code-injection path that could be triggered if bug JSON files were ever sourced from an untrusted location. | Confirmed pattern_matcher.py:369 `ast.parse(source_path, mode='eval')` grafts bug-JSON expressions into student files later run by pytest. | load-dotenv-override | security | engine/main.py:36 | Line 36: 'load_dotenv(override=True)' at module import time (before any CLI command runs). Any .env file in the current working directory can override already-set environment variables. | editor-env-code-execution | security | engine/main.py:310 | Lines 310 and 326: 'editor = os.getenv("EDITOR", os.getenv("VISUAL", "nano"))' then 'result = subprocess.run([editor, temp_path])'. The EDITOR environment variable is user-controlled and is executed directly without validation. If EDITOR is set to an arbitrary executable (e.g., a script that exfiltrates the justify answer written to temp_path), that executable runs with the same privileges as the engine. This follows the Unix convention for editor invocation (git commit uses the same pattern), so the risk only materialises if EDITOR is already malicious — but the engine never warns the user that EDITOR will be executed, and the engine's .env override (see load-dotenv-override) could hypothetically set EDITOR. | Confirmed main.py:310 `editor=os.getenv('EDITOR',...,'nano')` and :326 `subprocess.run([editor, temp_path])`. | rlimit-as-process-wide-side-effect | design_defect | tests/test_tokenizer.py:23 | Lines 19-36: The `memory_limit` decorator calls `resource.setrlimit(resource.RLIMIT_AS, (process.memory_info().rss + max_mem, -1))`. | ast-hardcoded-softmax-fallback | design_defect | engine/ast_harden/generic_injector.py:109 | Line 109: `canonicalizer = Canonicalizer(target_function=target_function if target_function else 'softmax')`. When `target_function` is not resolved (i.e. the bug definition does not specify it or the function detection yields None), the `Canonicalizer` defaults to treating the target as a `softmax` function. This causes the canonicalizer to rename variables according to softmax semantics even for non-softmax functions (e.g., `cross_entropy`, `rope`, `multihead_attention`), producing a canonical AST that does not reflect the actual function's variable roles. Pattern matching will then silently fail to find the injection point (`replacements_made == 0`) or, worse, match the wrong AST node. | Confirmed: generic_injector.py:109 'target_function if target_function else "softmax"'. All curricula bug JSONs specify target_function, so the falsy branch is never exercised in production. Latent defensive code smell; info. |

      "evidence": "The docstring at line 4 claims 'Build: O(n), Query: O(1), Space: O(n)' but the implementation `sumRange(nums, left, right)` at line 7 rebuilds the entire prefix array on every call: `prefix = [0] * (len(nums) + 1)` / `for i in range(len(nums)): prefix[i+1] = prefix[i] + nums[i]` (lines 25-27).
